From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.2, F32.3\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: What brings me in is… my sleep, mainly. It’s gotten completely unmoored, is the best way I can put it. At first I’d sleep maybe two or three hours and then I’d be wide awake, not tired at all, sitting there drafting plans and feeling like I was ready to go, like the day should start immediately even if it was still the middle of the night.\n\nBut now it’s changed into something worse. I’m awake before dawn, just lying there with my stomach tight, and I don’t feel rested. It’s like my body won’t settle. I keep thinking, if I could just sleep normally again, maybe I could get my footing, but right now it feels like sleep has stopped doing what it’s supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about things during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it worrying, exactly. I mean, I do have thoughts going around and around in my head during the day, but “worry” makes it sound like I’m calmly sitting there thinking through problems, and that’s not quite it.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it as a separate thing, like, “Oh, I’m worrying too much.” It’s more that my mind feels busy and hard to settle, and then I only realize later how tense I’ve been. But if you mean am I sitting there all day imagining terrible things happening or making long lists of fears, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so, actually. That’s one of the things that’s been… unsettling, because I used to be able to disappear into things. I could spend a whole afternoon at the museum, just moving from room to room and letting myself get absorbed. Or I’d take my camera out and lose track of time, noticing light on buildings or people’s hands or whatever caught my eye.\n\nAnd cooking too — I used to enjoy that process, planning something, chopping, tasting, fussing over it. Even bridge, sitting at the table, keeping track of the play, the conversation around it. Those things used to feel like me.\n\nLately I look at the camera, or I think about going to the museum, or I see the bridge table, and I just feel… nothing. Not even dislike, exactly. Just this blankness, and then the immediate thought of how much effort it would take to pretend I’m interested. Like I’d have to perform enjoyment rather than actually feel it. It’s hard to explain, but yes, the pleasure has mostly gone out of things I used to care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel afraid or anxious for no specific reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. “Afraid” feels a little strong, maybe, because I’m not walking around thinking there’s some particular danger, or that something specific is about to happen. It’s not like I can point to one thing and say, yes, that’s what I’m anxious about.\n\nBut I do get this… unsettled feeling. Sometimes it’s just there in my body before I’ve even attached any thought to it. I’ll be sitting in a chair or making tea or just standing in the kitchen, and there’s this tightness, this sense that I should be bracing for something, but I couldn’t tell you what. And then of course once I notice it, I start trying to explain it to myself, which doesn’t really help.\n\nSo, I don’t know if that counts as anxiety for no specific reason. It isn’t always fear in the usual sense, but yes, there are times when I feel keyed up or uneasy without being able to name a reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “sad” most of the day. That word makes me think of crying, or feeling grief, or having one clear feeling I can point to, and it isn’t always like that.\n\nA lot of the day feels heavy, though. Flat, maybe. I can be sitting there and it’s not that I’m thinking, “I’m so sad,” exactly, but there’s this low, dragged-down feeling over everything. And if someone asked me in the moment, I might even say I’m fine, because I’m not always visibly upset. But if I look back over the day, there’s not much lightness in it.\n\nSo… depressed, maybe, yes, if you mean that sort of ongoing weight or dullness. But I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, even though it does take up a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. I know I touched on it already, but it’s been one of the clearer changes, honestly.\n\nI used to be able to spend whole afternoons at the museum and feel completely absorbed, almost like the rest of the day disappeared. Or I’d take my camera out and just wander, looking for small things — light on a wall, someone’s posture, the way a street corner looked at a certain hour. Cooking was like that too, and even bridge, the rhythm of the game and the conversation around the table.\n\nLately, though, I’ll look at my camera, or think about going to the museum, or see the bridge table, and there’s just… nothing. Not anticipation, not curiosity, not even irritation exactly. Just blank. And then what I do feel is the weight of how much effort it would take to act as if I were enjoying it. Like I’d have to perform being interested. So yes, over the past couple of weeks, the pleasure has really dropped off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I have. It’s hard to say out loud, because I don’t want to alarm anyone or make it sound like I’m trying to be dramatic, but there have been mornings when I wake up—or before I’m fully awake, really—and I think it would have been a relief if I simply hadn’t woken up at all.\n\nIt isn’t always a fully formed thought like, “I’m going to do something,” but it’s there in this very dark, quiet way. Just the idea of not having to keep going through the day, not having to face myself, feels like relief. And then I get scared by the fact that I can even think that.\n\nIt frightened me enough that I told my wife I didn’t think I should be left alone. That was… not easy to say to her. But I didn’t trust the space I was in, and I thought it was better to say it out loud than pretend it wasn’t happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest in activities for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than two weeks, yes.\n\nI know I’ve mentioned the museum and the camera already, but that’s really the clearest way I can explain it. Those things used to pull me in without any effort. I could spend an entire afternoon in a museum and not notice the time passing, or go out with my camera and get absorbed in small details. Cooking was like that too. Even bridge — I used to enjoy the game, the thinking, the bit of company around it.\n\nNow I can look at the camera sitting there, or think about going to the museum, or sit near the bridge table, and I feel… nothing. Not even real dislike. Just this blankness. And then almost immediately I think about how much energy it would take to pretend I was interested, or to act like I was enjoying myself the way I used to.\n\nSo yes, it’s been going on longer than two weeks. It’s not just a bad day or two.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, and I don’t have a clear number I can give you.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed in any reliable way. Sometimes I think my trousers feel a bit looser or tighter, but then I wonder if I’m just paying too much attention or imagining it because you’re asking. So… no significant weight change that I can confidently point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a lack of energy or fatigue most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I do feel tired a lot of the time, but I don’t know if I can honestly say it’s “most of the day” in the way you mean it.\n\nSome days I sit down and feel like I could just stay there indefinitely, but then I wonder whether that’s actual physical fatigue or more that I don’t want to start anything. It’s hard to separate the two. At my age, too, I sometimes second-guess myself — I think, well, maybe everyone feels a bit slower or more worn out than they used to.\n\nI haven’t noticed something like suddenly needing to nap all day, or collapsing from exhaustion, if that’s what you’re asking. It’s more vague than that. A heaviness, maybe. But I don’t have a clear, confident yes-or-no answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your depression is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t call it mild. I wish I could, but it’s gone well past just feeling low and still carrying on as usual.\n\nIt’s interfering with ordinary things now — things I used to manage without thinking. My wife has had to take over the finances, which is… embarrassing to say, because I’ve always been capable with that sort of thing. Bills, accounts, keeping track of what’s due — that used to be part of my routine. Now it’s like I can’t reliably handle it, and she’s had to step in.\n\nAnd she’s been staying with me more, really watching over me, for safety. I don’t like needing that. I don’t like admitting that she feels she has to do that.\n\nI’ve also stopped showing up reliably for things. The museum, the arts council, friends — I used to be dependable. If I said I’d be somewhere, I’d be there. Now I cancel, or I just don’t make it, or I can’t bring myself to answer people properly. So it’s not mild. It’s affecting my life in a very real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your low mood or depression is present nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to pin that down. It feels like it’s there a lot, but “nearly every day” makes me hesitate, because I don’t want to overstate it or give you an answer that sounds neater than it really is.\n\nThere are days where I wake up and it’s just… already there, before anything has even happened. And then other days I might not notice it in the same obvious way, or I’ll get distracted for a while, and I think, well, does that count as it not being present? Or is it still there underneath and I’m just not paying attention to it for an hour?\n\nSo I suppose I’d say it’s frequent enough that it feels like part of the background of my life at the moment, but I can’t honestly give you a precise “yes, every single day” sort of answer. It’s hard for me to judge cleanly.\n\nDoctor: How many days in the past week have you felt depressed or had a low mood?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean number, but I’m honestly not sure. If I try to look back over the last week, the days sort of blur together a bit.\n\nI’d say it was several days, probably most of them in some form, but I don’t know whether each day would count as “depressed” in the way you’re asking. Some days it’s very obvious, and other days it’s more like a dull background feeling that I only notice when I stop moving or when things get quiet.\n\nSo… I can’t confidently say, “It was exactly five” or “exactly six.” It’s been there enough that the week feels colored by it, but I don’t have a precise count.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the low mood and loss of interest in activities?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months now, I think. Not just a few bad weeks. It really seemed to settle in after those plans fell apart, and since then I’ve been sort of withdrawn and stuck in this low, guilty place.\n\nAt first I told myself I was just disappointed and that I’d pull myself together once the dust settled, but it didn’t lift. If anything, it became the way I was living day to day — not taking interest in things I used to care about, pulling back from people, feeling weighed down by it. So yes, roughly nine months. That’s the best estimate I can give.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your depression is severely impairing your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have to say yes, even though part of me wants to soften it or explain it away. It’s not just that I feel low and still manage everything quietly. It has really changed what I can do day to day.\n\nMy wife has had to take over the finances, which is hard for me to admit. I used to be the one who could keep track of things, pay attention to details, make sure bills and accounts were handled properly. Now it feels like too much, and she’s had to step in because I’m not reliable with it.\n\nAnd she stays with me for safety, too. That sounds terrible to say out loud, but it’s true. I don’t like needing that kind of watching over, especially at my age. It makes me feel ashamed and dependent, but I can see why she feels she has to.\n\nI’ve also stopped showing up reliably for things I used to be involved in — the museum, the arts council, even seeing friends. I don’t mean that I miss one thing here or there. It’s become a pattern. People probably don’t know whether I’m coming anymore, and honestly sometimes I don’t either. I just withdraw or fail to follow through.\n\nSo yes, I’d call it severely impairing. It’s not just internal anymore. It’s affecting my responsibilities, my marriage, my commitments, and my friendships.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… both, oddly enough. Or at least that’s how it looks from the outside, according to my wife.\n\nShe says there are times when I’m pacing through the house, moving from room to room, carrying papers around or shuffling them like I’m late for some board meeting I’ve forgotten about. I’m not always accomplishing anything when I do it. It’s more like I can’t quite settle, and I keep picking things up, putting them down, checking them, rearranging them.\n\nAnd then other times it’s the opposite. I’ll sit at the kitchen table and just sort of… freeze there. My wife will ask me something simple, nothing complicated, and it takes me a long time to answer. Not because I’m trying to be difficult. It’s like the question has to travel a long distance before I can respond. I can feel myself being slow, but I can’t always make myself speed up.\n\nSo yes, I have noticed it, but she notices it more clearly than I do. It seems to swing between this restless pacing and then sitting so still that I barely seem present.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I realized at first, actually.\n\nMy wife notices it most. She says sometimes I’ll sit at the kitchen table almost completely still, and when she asks me something simple, it takes me a long time to answer. I can hear her, but it’s like I’m delayed somehow, like I have to pull the words up through mud before I can say anything. It’s embarrassing, because I know it probably looks like I’m ignoring her or not trying, but I’m not.\n\nAnd then other times I’m not slow exactly — I’ll be up pacing through the house, shuffling papers around, almost like I’m late for some board meeting I’ve forgotten about. But when I stop and look at what I’m doing, I’m not really getting anywhere. It’s just this restless moving around.\n\nSo yes, especially with speaking slowly or taking too long to respond. That part has definitely been happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

I’m not sure I can point to a whole day like that, no. I mean, “completely free” is a pretty strong phrase, and I don’t want to overstate it either way.

There may be moments where I’m distracted or where things feel a little lighter for an hour or two, but if you’re asking whether I’ve had an entire day in the past couple of weeks where I woke up and went through the day without that depressed feeling being there somewhere in the background… I don’t think so.

It’s more like it changes in intensity. Some parts of the day are worse than others, and sometimes I can almost forget it briefly if something occupies me, but it hasn’t really lifted in a clean way. I wouldn’t say I’ve felt completely free of it.

It’s not like I can point to one thing and say, yes, that’s what I’m anxious about.\n\nBut I do get this… unsettled feeling. Sometimes it’s just there in my body before I’ve even attached any thought to it. I’ll be sitting in a chair or making tea or just standing in the kitchen, and there’s this tightness, this sense that I should be bracing for something, but I couldn’t tell you what. And then of course once I notice it, I start trying to explain it to myself, which doesn’t really help.\n\nSo, I don’t know if that counts as anxiety for no specific reason. It isn’t always fear in the usual sense, but yes, there are times when I feel keyed up or uneasy without being able to name a reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “sad” most of the day. Definitely. More than two weeks, yes.\n\nI know I’ve mentioned the museum and the camera already, but that’s really the clearest way I can explain it. Those things used to pull me in without any effort. I could spend an entire afternoon in a museum and not notice the time passing, or go out with my camera and get absorbed in small details. Cooking was like that too. Even bridge — I used to enjoy the game, the thinking, the bit of company around it.\n\nNow I can look at the camera sitting there, or think about going to the museum, or sit near the bridge table, and I feel… nothing. Not even real dislike. Just this blankness. It’s not just a bad day or two.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, and I don’t have a clear number I can give you.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed in any reliable way. Sometimes I think my trousers feel a bit looser or tighter, but then I wonder if I’m just paying too much attention or imagining it because you’re asking. So… no significant weight change that I can confidently point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a lack of energy or fatigue most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. It’s hard to separate the two. At my age, too, I sometimes second-guess myself — I think, well, maybe everyone feels a bit slower or more worn out than they used to.\n\nI haven’t noticed something like suddenly needing to nap all day, or collapsing from exhaustion, if that’s what you’re asking. It’s more vague than that. A heaviness, maybe. But I don’t have a clear, confident yes-or-no answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your depression is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t call it mild. I wish I could, but it’s gone well past just feeling low and still carrying on as usual.\n\nIt’s interfering with ordinary things now — things I used to manage without thinking. My wife has had to take over the finances, which is… embarrassing to say, because I’ve always been capable with that sort of thing. Bills, accounts, keeping track of what’s due — that used to be part of my routine. Now it’s like I can’t reliably handle it, and she’s had to step in.\n\nAnd she’s been staying with me more, really watching over me, for safety. I don’t like needing that. I don’t like admitting that she feels she has to do that.\n\nI’ve also stopped showing up reliably for things. The museum, the arts council, friends — I used to be dependable. If I said I’d be somewhere, I’d be there. Now I cancel, or I just don’t make it, or I can’t bring myself to answer people properly. So it’s not mild. It’s affecting my life in a very real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your low mood or depression is present nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to pin that down. It feels like it’s there a lot, but “nearly every day” makes me hesitate, because I don’t want to overstate it or give you an answer that sounds neater than it really is.\n\nThere are days where I wake up and it’s just… already there, before anything has even happened. And then other days I might not notice it in the same obvious way, or I’ll get distracted for a while, and I think, well, does that count as it not being present? Or is it still there underneath and I’m just not paying attention to it for an hour?\n\nSo I suppose I’d say it’s frequent enough that it feels like part of the background of my life at the moment, but I can’t honestly give you a precise “yes, every single day” sort of answer. It’s hard for me to judge cleanly.\n\nDoctor: How many days in the past week have you felt depressed or had a low mood?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean number, but I’m honestly not sure. If I try to look back over the last week, the days sort of blur together a bit.\n\nI’d say it was several days, probably most of them in some form, but I don’t know whether each day would count as “depressed” in the way you’re asking. Some days it’s very obvious, and other days it’s more like a dull background feeling that I only notice when I stop moving or when things get quiet.\n\nSo… I can’t confidently say, “It was exactly five” or “exactly six.” It’s been there enough that the week feels colored by it, but I don’t have a precise count.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the low mood and loss of interest in activities?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months now, I think. Not just a few bad weeks. It really seemed to settle in after those plans fell apart, and since then I’ve been sort of withdrawn and stuck in this low, guilty place.\n\nAt first I told myself I was just disappointed and that I’d pull myself together once the dust settled, but it didn’t lift. If anything, it became the way I was living day to day — not taking interest in things I used to care about, pulling back from people, feeling weighed down by it. So yes, roughly nine months. That’s the best estimate I can give.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your depression is severely impairing your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have to say yes, even though part of me wants to soften it or explain it away. It’s not just that I feel low and still manage everything quietly. It has really changed what I can do day to day.\n\nMy wife has had to take over the finances, which is hard for me to admit. I used to be the one who could keep track of things, pay attention to details, make sure bills and accounts were handled properly. Now it feels like too much, and she’s had to step in because I’m not reliable with it.\n\nAnd she stays with me for safety, too. That sounds terrible to say out loud, but it’s true. I don’t like needing that kind of watching over, especially at my age. It makes me feel ashamed and dependent, but I can see why she feels she has to.\n\nI’ve also stopped showing up reliably for things I used to be involved in — the museum, the arts council, even seeing friends. I don’t mean that I miss one thing here or there. It’s become a pattern. People probably don’t know whether I’m coming anymore, and honestly sometimes I don’t either. I just withdraw or fail to follow through.\n\nSo yes, I’d call it severely impairing. It’s not just internal anymore. It’s affecting my responsibilities, my marriage, my commitments, and my friendships.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… both, oddly enough. Or at least that’s how it looks from the outside, according to my wife.\n\nShe says there are times when I’m pacing through the house, moving from room to room, carrying papers around or shuffling them like I’m late for some board meeting I’ve forgotten about. I’m not always accomplishing anything when I do it. It’s more like I can’t quite settle, and I keep picking things up, putting them down, checking them, rearranging them.\n\nAnd then other times it’s the opposite. I’ll sit at the kitchen table and just sort of… freeze there. My wife will ask me something simple, nothing complicated, and it takes me a long time to answer. Not because I’m trying to be difficult. It’s like the question has to travel a long distance before I can respond. I can feel myself being slow, but I can’t always make myself speed up.\n\nSo yes, I have noticed it, but she notices it more clearly than I do. It seems to swing between this restless pacing and then sitting so still that I barely seem present.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I realized at first, actually.\n\nMy wife notices it most. She says sometimes I’ll sit at the kitchen table almost completely still, and when she asks me something simple, it takes me a long time to answer. I can hear her, but it’s like I’m delayed somehow, like I have to pull the words up through mud before I can say anything. It’s embarrassing, because I know it probably looks like I’m ignoring her or not trying, but I’m not.\n\nAnd then other times I’m not slow exactly — I’ll be up pacing through the house, shuffling papers around, almost like I’m late for some board meeting I’ve forgotten about. But when I stop and look at what I’m doing, I’m not really getting anywhere. It’s just this restless moving around.\n\nSo yes, especially with speaking slowly or taking too long to respond. That part has definitely been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a whole day like that, no. I mean, “completely free” is a pretty strong phrase, and I don’t want to overstate it either way.\n\nThere may be moments where I’m distracted or where things feel a little lighter for an hour or two, but if you’re asking whether I’ve had an entire day in the past couple of weeks where I woke up and went through the day without that depressed feeling being there somewhere in the background… I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more like it changes in intensity. Some parts of the day are worse than others, and sometimes I can almost forget it briefly if something occupies me, but it hasn’t really lifted in a clean way. I wouldn’t say I’ve felt completely free of it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll sit at the kitchen table and just sort of… freeze there. My wife will ask me something simple, nothing complicated, and it takes me a long time to answer. Not because I’m trying to be difficult. It’s like the question has to travel a long distance before I can respond. I can feel myself being slow, but I can’t always make myself speed up.\n\nSo yes, I have noticed it, but she notices it more clearly than I do. It seems to swing between this restless pacing and then sitting so still that I barely seem present.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I realized at first, actually.\n\nMy wife notices it most. She says sometimes I’ll sit at the kitchen table almost completely still, and when she asks me something simple, it takes me a long time to answer. I can hear her, but it’s like I’m delayed somehow, like I have to pull the words up through mud before I can say anything. It’s embarrassing, because I know it probably looks like I’m ignoring her or not trying, but I’m not.\n\nAnd then other times I’m not slow exactly — I’ll be up pacing through the house, shuffling papers around, almost like I’m late for some board meeting I’ve forgotten about. But when I stop and look at what I’m doing, I’m not really getting anywhere. It’s just this restless moving around.\n\nSo yes, especially with speaking slowly or taking too long to respond. That part has definitely been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a whole day like that, no. I mean, “completely free” is a pretty strong phrase, and I don’t want to overstate it either way.\n\nThere may be moments where I’m distracted or where things feel a little lighter for an hour or two, but if you’re asking whether I’ve had an entire day in the past couple of weeks where I woke up and went through the day without that depressed feeling being there somewhere in the background… I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more like it changes in intensity. Some parts of the day are worse than others, and sometimes I can almost forget it briefly if something occupies me, but it hasn’t really lifted in a clean way. I wouldn’t say I’ve felt completely free of it.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced any periods in the past two weeks where your depressive symptoms were significantly worse than others?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know if I can divide it up that neatly. It hasn’t felt like, say, Tuesday was dramatically different from Wednesday in a way I could mark on a calendar.

There are definitely parts of days that feel heavier than others, but I’m not sure I’d call them distinct “periods” where everything got significantly worse. It’s more like the whole thing is there, and it shifts around a bit — sometimes more in the foreground, sometimes more like background noise that still affects everything.

I wish I had kept better track, honestly, because when you ask it that way I find myself trying to reconstruct the last two weeks and it all blurs together. So… I’d say I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern of certain periods being much worse than the rest.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: What brings me in is… my sleep, mainly. It’s gotten completely unmoored, is the best way I can put it. At first I’d sleep maybe two or three hours and then I’d be wide awake, not tired at all, sitting there drafting plans and feeling like I was ready to go, like the day should start immediately even if it was still the middle of the night.\n\nBut now it’s changed into something worse. I’m awake before dawn, just lying there with my stomach tight, and I don’t feel rested. It’s like my body won’t settle. Like I’d have to perform being interested. So yes, over the past couple of weeks, the pleasure has really dropped off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I have. It’s hard to say out loud, because I don’t want to alarm anyone or make it sound like I’m trying to be dramatic, but there have been mornings when I wake up—or before I’m fully awake, really—and I think it would have been a relief if I simply hadn’t woken up at all.\n\nIt isn’t always a fully formed thought like, “I’m going to do something,” but it’s there in this very dark, quiet way. Just the idea of not having to keep going through the day, not having to face myself, feels like relief. I could spend an entire afternoon in a museum and not notice the time passing, or go out with my camera and get absorbed in small details. Cooking was like that too. I’m awake before dawn, just lying there with my stomach tight, and I don’t feel rested. It’s like my body won’t settle. I keep thinking, if I could just sleep normally again, maybe I could get my footing, but right now it feels like sleep has stopped doing what it’s supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about things during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it worrying, exactly. I mean, I do have thoughts going around and around in my head during the day, but “worry” makes it sound like I’m calmly sitting there thinking through problems, and that’s not quite it.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it as a separate thing, like, “Oh, I’m worrying too much.” It’s more that my mind feels busy and hard to settle, and then I only realize later how tense I’ve been. But if you mean am I sitting there all day imagining terrible things happening or making long lists of fears, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so, actually. That’s one of the things that’s been… unsettling, because I used to be able to disappear into things. I could spend a whole afternoon at the museum, just moving from room to room and letting myself get absorbed. Or I’d take my camera out and lose track of time, noticing light on buildings or people’s hands or whatever caught my eye.\n\nAnd cooking too — I used to enjoy that process, planning something, chopping, tasting, fussing over it. Even bridge, sitting at the table, keeping track of the play, the conversation around it. Those things used to feel like me.\n\nLately I look at the camera, or I think about going to the museum, or I see the bridge table, and I just feel… nothing. Not even dislike, exactly. Just this blankness, and then the immediate thought of how much effort it would take to pretend I’m interested. Like I’d have to perform enjoyment rather than actually feel it. It’s hard to explain, but yes, the pleasure has mostly gone out of things I used to care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel afraid or anxious for no specific reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. “Afraid” feels a little strong, maybe, because I’m not walking around thinking there’s some particular danger, or that something specific is about to happen. It’s not like I can point to one thing and say, yes, that’s what I’m anxious about.\n\nBut I do get this… unsettled feeling. Sometimes it’s just there in my body before I’ve even attached any thought to it. I’ll be sitting in a chair or making tea or just standing in the kitchen, and there’s this tightness, this sense that I should be bracing for something, but I couldn’t tell you what. And then of course once I notice it, I start trying to explain it to myself, which doesn’t really help.\n\nSo, I don’t know if that counts as anxiety for no specific reason. It isn’t always fear in the usual sense, but yes, there are times when I feel keyed up or uneasy without being able to name a reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “sad” most of the day. That word makes me think of crying, or feeling grief, or having one clear feeling I can point to, and it isn’t always like that.\n\nA lot of the day feels heavy, though. Flat, maybe. I can be sitting there and it’s not that I’m thinking, “I’m so sad,” exactly, but there’s this low, dragged-down feeling over everything. And if someone asked me in the moment, I might even say I’m fine, because I’m not always visibly upset. But if I look back over the day, there’s not much lightness in it.\n\nSo… depressed, maybe, yes, if you mean that sort of ongoing weight or dullness. But I hesitate because I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, even though it does take up a lot of the day.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed a decrease in pleasure from activities that you used to enjoy over the past two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely. I know I touched on it already, but it’s been one of the clearer changes, honestly.\n\nI used to be able to spend whole afternoons at the museum and feel completely absorbed, almost like the rest of the day disappeared. Or I’d take my camera out and just wander, looking for small things — light on a wall, someone’s posture, the way a street corner looked at a certain hour. Cooking was like that too, and even bridge, the rhythm of the game and the conversation around the table.\n\nLately, though, I’ll look at my camera, or think about going to the museum, or see the bridge table, and there’s just… nothing. Not anticipation, not curiosity, not even irritation exactly. Just blank. And then what I do feel is the weight of how much effort it would take to act as if I were enjoying it. Like I’d have to perform being interested. So yes, over the past couple of weeks, the pleasure has really dropped off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I have. It’s hard to say out loud, because I don’t want to alarm anyone or make it sound like I’m trying to be dramatic, but there have been mornings when I wake up—or before I’m fully awake, really—and I think it would have been a relief if I simply hadn’t woken up at all.\n\nIt isn’t always a fully formed thought like, “I’m going to do something,” but it’s there in this very dark, quiet way. Just the idea of not having to keep going through the day, not having to face myself, feels like relief. And then I get scared by the fact that I can even think that.\n\nIt frightened me enough that I told my wife I didn’t think I should be left alone. That was… not easy to say to her. But I didn’t trust the space I was in, and I thought it was better to say it out loud than pretend it wasn’t happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest in activities for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than two weeks, yes.\n\nI know I’ve mentioned the museum and the camera already, but that’s really the clearest way I can explain it. Those things used to pull me in without any effort. I could spend an entire afternoon in a museum and not notice the time passing, or go out with my camera and get absorbed in small details. Cooking was like that too. Even bridge — I used to enjoy the game, the thinking, the bit of company around it.\n\nNow I can look at the camera sitting there, or think about going to the museum, or sit near the bridge table, and I feel… nothing. Not even real dislike. Just this blankness. And then almost immediately I think about how much energy it would take to pretend I was interested, or to act like I was enjoying myself the way I used to.\n\nSo yes, it’s been going on longer than two weeks. It’s not just a bad day or two.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, and I don’t have a clear number I can give you.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed in any reliable way. Sometimes I think my trousers feel a bit looser or tighter, but then I wonder if I’m just paying too much attention or imagining it because you’re asking. So… no significant weight change that I can confidently point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a lack of energy or fatigue most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I do feel tired a lot of the time, but I don’t know if I can honestly say it’s “most of the day” in the way you mean it.\n\nSome days I sit down and feel like I could just stay there indefinitely, but then I wonder whether that’s actual physical fatigue or more that I don’t want to start anything. It’s hard to separate the two. At my age, too, I sometimes second-guess myself — I think, well, maybe everyone feels a bit slower or more worn out than they used to.\n\nI haven’t noticed something like suddenly needing to nap all day, or collapsing from exhaustion, if that’s what you’re asking. It’s more vague than that. A heaviness, maybe. But I don’t have a clear, confident yes-or-no answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your depression is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t call it mild. I wish I could, but it’s gone well past just feeling low and still carrying on as usual.\n\nIt’s interfering with ordinary things now — things I used to manage without thinking. My wife has had to take over the finances, which is… embarrassing to say, because I’ve always been capable with that sort of thing. Bills, accounts, keeping track of what’s due — that used to be part of my routine. Now it’s like I can’t reliably handle it, and she’s had to step in.\n\nAnd she’s been staying with me more, really watching over me, for safety. I don’t like needing that. I don’t like admitting that she feels she has to do that.\n\nI’ve also stopped showing up reliably for things. The museum, the arts council, friends — I used to be dependable. If I said I’d be somewhere, I’d be there. Now I cancel, or I just don’t make it, or I can’t bring myself to answer people properly. So it’s not mild. It’s affecting my life in a very real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your low mood or depression is present nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to pin that down. It feels like it’s there a lot, but “nearly every day” makes me hesitate, because I don’t want to overstate it or give you an answer that sounds neater than it really is.\n\nThere are days where I wake up and it’s just… already there, before anything has even happened. And then other days I might not notice it in the same obvious way, or I’ll get distracted for a while, and I think, well, does that count as it not being present? Or is it still there underneath and I’m just not paying attention to it for an hour?\n\nSo I suppose I’d say it’s frequent enough that it feels like part of the background of my life at the moment, but I can’t honestly give you a precise “yes, every single day” sort of answer. It’s hard for me to judge cleanly.\n\nDoctor: How many days in the past week have you felt depressed or had a low mood?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean number, but I’m honestly not sure. If I try to look back over the last week, the days sort of blur together a bit.\n\nI’d say it was several days, probably most of them in some form, but I don’t know whether each day would count as “depressed” in the way you’re asking. Some days it’s very obvious, and other days it’s more like a dull background feeling that I only notice when I stop moving or when things get quiet.\n\nSo… I can’t confidently say, “It was exactly five” or “exactly six.” It’s been there enough that the week feels colored by it, but I don’t have a precise count.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the low mood and loss of interest in activities?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months now, I think. Not just a few bad weeks. It really seemed to settle in after those plans fell apart, and since then I’ve been sort of withdrawn and stuck in this low, guilty place.\n\nAt first I told myself I was just disappointed and that I’d pull myself together once the dust settled, but it didn’t lift. If anything, it became the way I was living day to day — not taking interest in things I used to care about, pulling back from people, feeling weighed down by it. So yes, roughly nine months. That’s the best estimate I can give.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your depression is severely impairing your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have to say yes, even though part of me wants to soften it or explain it away. It’s not just that I feel low and still manage everything quietly. It has really changed what I can do day to day.\n\nMy wife has had to take over the finances, which is hard for me to admit. I used to be the one who could keep track of things, pay attention to details, make sure bills and accounts were handled properly. Now it feels like too much, and she’s had to step in because I’m not reliable with it.\n\nAnd she stays with me for safety, too. That sounds terrible to say out loud, but it’s true. I don’t like needing that kind of watching over, especially at my age. It makes me feel ashamed and dependent, but I can see why she feels she has to.\n\nI’ve also stopped showing up reliably for things I used to be involved in — the museum, the arts council, even seeing friends. I don’t mean that I miss one thing here or there. It’s become a pattern. People probably don’t know whether I’m coming anymore, and honestly sometimes I don’t either. I just withdraw or fail to follow through.\n\nSo yes, I’d call it severely impairing. It’s not just internal anymore. It’s affecting my responsibilities, my marriage, my commitments, and my friendships.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… both, oddly enough. Or at least that’s how it looks from the outside, according to my wife.\n\nShe says there are times when I’m pacing through the house, moving from room to room, carrying papers around or shuffling them like I’m late for some board meeting I’ve forgotten about. I’m not always accomplishing anything when I do it. It’s more like I can’t quite settle, and I keep picking things up, putting them down, checking them, rearranging them.\n\nAnd then other times it’s the opposite. I’ll sit at the kitchen table and just sort of… freeze there. My wife will ask me something simple, nothing complicated, and it takes me a long time to answer. Not because I’m trying to be difficult. It’s like the question has to travel a long distance before I can respond. I can feel myself being slow, but I can’t always make myself speed up.\n\nSo yes, I have noticed it, but she notices it more clearly than I do. It seems to swing between this restless pacing and then sitting so still that I barely seem present.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I realized at first, actually.\n\nMy wife notices it most. She says sometimes I’ll sit at the kitchen table almost completely still, and when she asks me something simple, it takes me a long time to answer. I can hear her, but it’s like I’m delayed somehow, like I have to pull the words up through mud before I can say anything. It’s embarrassing, because I know it probably looks like I’m ignoring her or not trying, but I’m not.\n\nAnd then other times I’m not slow exactly — I’ll be up pacing through the house, shuffling papers around, almost like I’m late for some board meeting I’ve forgotten about. But when I stop and look at what I’m doing, I’m not really getting anywhere. It’s just this restless moving around.\n\nSo yes, especially with speaking slowly or taking too long to respond. That part has definitely been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a whole day like that, no. I mean, “completely free” is a pretty strong phrase, and I don’t want to overstate it either way.\n\nThere may be moments where I’m distracted or where things feel a little lighter for an hour or two, but if you’re asking whether I’ve had an entire day in the past couple of weeks where I woke up and went through the day without that depressed feeling being there somewhere in the background… I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more like it changes in intensity. Some parts of the day are worse than others, and sometimes I can almost forget it briefly if something occupies me, but it hasn’t really lifted in a clean way. I wouldn’t say I’ve felt completely free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the past two weeks where your depressive symptoms were significantly worse than others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can divide it up that neatly. It hasn’t felt like, say, Tuesday was dramatically different from Wednesday in a way I could mark on a calendar.\n\nThere are definitely parts of days that feel heavier than others, but I’m not sure I’d call them distinct “periods” where everything got significantly worse. It’s more like the whole thing is there, and it shifts around a bit — sometimes more in the foreground, sometimes more like background noise that still affects everything.\n\nI wish I had kept better track, honestly, because when you ask it that way I find myself trying to reconstruct the last two weeks and it all blurs together. So… I’d say I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern of certain periods being much worse than the rest.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.

There have been mornings where I wake up and almost immediately think it would have been easier if I just hadn’t woken up at all. Not in a dramatic way, exactly — more like this heavy sense of relief at the idea of not having to keep going through the day. And that scared me, because it felt too close to something I shouldn’t be sitting with by myself.

I told my wife, actually. I told her I didn’t think I should be left alone. That was… hard to say to her, but I was frightened by where my mind was going.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: What brings me in is… my sleep, mainly. It’s gotten completely unmoored, is the best way I can put it. At first I’d sleep maybe two or three hours and then I’d be wide awake, not tired at all, sitting there drafting plans and feeling like I was ready to go, like the day should start immediately even if it was still the middle of the night.\n\nBut now it’s changed into something worse. I’m awake before dawn, just lying there with my stomach tight, and I don’t feel rested. It’s like my body won’t settle. I keep thinking, if I could just sleep normally again, maybe I could get my footing, but right now it feels like sleep has stopped doing what it’s supposed to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about things during the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it worrying, exactly. I mean, I do have thoughts going around and around in my head during the day, but “worry” makes it sound like I’m calmly sitting there thinking through problems, and that’s not quite it.\n\nI guess I haven’t really noticed it as a separate thing, like, “Oh, I’m worrying too much.” It’s more that my mind feels busy and hard to settle, and then I only realize later how tense I’ve been. But if you mean am I sitting there all day imagining terrible things happening or making long lists of fears, I’m not sure. I wouldn’t want to overstate it.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that you're enjoying things you used to like?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so, actually. That’s one of the things that’s been… unsettling, because I used to be able to disappear into things. I could spend a whole afternoon at the museum, just moving from room to room and letting myself get absorbed. Or I’d take my camera out and lose track of time, noticing light on buildings or people’s hands or whatever caught my eye.\n\nAnd cooking too — I used to enjoy that process, planning something, chopping, tasting, fussing over it. Just this blankness, and then the immediate thought of how much effort it would take to pretend I’m interested. Like I’d have to perform enjoyment rather than actually feel it. It’s hard to explain, but yes, the pleasure has mostly gone out of things I used to care about.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel afraid or anxious for no specific reason?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. “Afraid” feels a little strong, maybe, because I’m not walking around thinking there’s some particular danger, or that something specific is about to happen. It’s not like I can point to one thing and say, yes, that’s what I’m anxious about.\n\nBut I do get this… unsettled feeling. Sometimes it’s just there in my body before I’ve even attached any thought to it. I’ll be sitting in a chair or making tea or just standing in the kitchen, and there’s this tightness, this sense that I should be bracing for something, but I couldn’t tell you what. And then of course once I notice it, I start trying to explain it to myself, which doesn’t really help.\n\nSo, I don’t know if that counts as anxiety for no specific reason. It isn’t always fear in the usual sense, but yes, there are times when I feel keyed up or uneasy without being able to name a reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “sad” most of the day. Not anticipation, not curiosity, not even irritation exactly. Just blank. And then what I do feel is the weight of how much effort it would take to act as if I were enjoying it. Like I’d have to perform being interested. So yes, over the past couple of weeks, the pleasure has really dropped off.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: Yes… I’m afraid I have. It’s hard to say out loud, because I don’t want to alarm anyone or make it sound like I’m trying to be dramatic, but there have been mornings when I wake up—or before I’m fully awake, really—and I think it would have been a relief if I simply hadn’t woken up at all.\n\nIt isn’t always a fully formed thought like, “I’m going to do something,” but it’s there in this very dark, quiet way. Just the idea of not having to keep going through the day, not having to face myself, feels like relief. And then I get scared by the fact that I can even think that.\n\nIt frightened me enough that I told my wife I didn’t think I should be left alone. That was… not easy to say to her. But I didn’t trust the space I was in, and I thought it was better to say it out loud than pretend it wasn’t happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest in activities for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than two weeks, yes.\n\nI know I’ve mentioned the museum and the camera already, but that’s really the clearest way I can explain it. Those things used to pull me in without any effort. I could spend an entire afternoon in a museum and not notice the time passing, or go out with my camera and get absorbed in small details. Cooking was like that too. Even bridge — I used to enjoy the game, the thinking, the bit of company around it.\n\nNow I can look at the camera sitting there, or think about going to the museum, or sit near the bridge table, and I feel… nothing. Not even real dislike. Just this blankness. And then almost immediately I think about how much energy it would take to pretend I was interested, or to act like I was enjoying myself the way I used to.\n\nSo yes, it’s been going on longer than two weeks. It’s not just a bad day or two.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, and I don’t have a clear number I can give you.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed in any reliable way. Sometimes I think my trousers feel a bit looser or tighter, but then I wonder if I’m just paying too much attention or imagining it because you’re asking. So… no significant weight change that I can confidently point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a lack of energy or fatigue most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. It’s more vague than that. A heaviness, maybe. If anything, it became the way I was living day to day — not taking interest in things I used to care about, pulling back from people, feeling weighed down by it. So yes, roughly nine months. That’s the best estimate I can give.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your depression is severely impairing your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have to say yes, even though part of me wants to soften it or explain it away. It’s not just that I feel low and still manage everything quietly. It has really changed what I can do day to day.\n\nMy wife has had to take over the finances, which is hard for me to admit. I used to be the one who could keep track of things, pay attention to details, make sure bills and accounts were handled properly. Now it feels like too much, and she’s had to step in because I’m not reliable with it.\n\nAnd she stays with me for safety, too. That sounds terrible to say out loud, but it’s true. I don’t like needing that kind of watching over, especially at my age. It makes me feel ashamed and dependent, but I can see why she feels she has to.\n\nI’ve also stopped showing up reliably for things I used to be involved in — the museum, the arts council, even seeing friends. I don’t mean that I miss one thing here or there. It’s become a pattern. People probably don’t know whether I’m coming anymore, and honestly sometimes I don’t either. I just withdraw or fail to follow through.\n\nSo yes, I’d call it severely impairing. It’s not just internal anymore. It’s affecting my responsibilities, my marriage, my commitments, and my friendships.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… both, oddly enough. I’m not always accomplishing anything when I do it. It’s more like I can’t quite settle, and I keep picking things up, putting them down, checking them, rearranging them.\n\nAnd then other times it’s the opposite. I’ll sit at the kitchen table and just sort of… freeze there. My wife will ask me something simple, nothing complicated, and it takes me a long time to answer. Not because I’m trying to be difficult. It’s like the question has to travel a long distance before I can respond. More than I realized at first, actually.\n\nMy wife notices it most. She says sometimes I’ll sit at the kitchen table almost completely still, and when she asks me something simple, it takes me a long time to answer. I can hear her, but it’s like I’m delayed somehow, like I have to pull the words up through mud before I can say anything. I mean, “completely free” is a pretty strong phrase, and I don’t want to overstate it either way.\n\nThere may be moments where I’m distracted or where things feel a little lighter for an hour or two, but if you’re asking whether I’ve had an entire day in the past couple of weeks where I woke up and went through the day without that depressed feeling being there somewhere in the background… I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more like it changes in intensity. Some parts of the day are worse than others, and sometimes I can almost forget it briefly if something occupies me, but it hasn’t really lifted in a clean way. It’s hard to say out loud, because I don’t want to alarm anyone or make it sound like I’m trying to be dramatic, but there have been mornings when I wake up—or before I’m fully awake, really—and I think it would have been a relief if I simply hadn’t woken up at all.\n\nIt isn’t always a fully formed thought like, “I’m going to do something,” but it’s there in this very dark, quiet way. Just the idea of not having to keep going through the day, not having to face myself, feels like relief. And then I get scared by the fact that I can even think that.\n\nIt frightened me enough that I told my wife I didn’t think I should be left alone. That was… not easy to say to her. But I didn’t trust the space I was in, and I thought it was better to say it out loud than pretend it wasn’t happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a loss of interest in activities for more than two weeks?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than two weeks, yes.\n\nI know I’ve mentioned the museum and the camera already, but that’s really the clearest way I can explain it. Those things used to pull me in without any effort. I could spend an entire afternoon in a museum and not notice the time passing, or go out with my camera and get absorbed in small details. Cooking was like that too. Even bridge — I used to enjoy the game, the thinking, the bit of company around it.\n\nNow I can look at the camera sitting there, or think about going to the museum, or sit near the bridge table, and I feel… nothing. Not even real dislike. Just this blankness. And then almost immediately I think about how much energy it would take to pretend I was interested, or to act like I was enjoying myself the way I used to.\n\nSo yes, it’s been going on longer than two weeks. It’s not just a bad day or two.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight recently?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, to be honest. I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, and I don’t have a clear number I can give you.\n\nMy clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed in any reliable way. Sometimes I think my trousers feel a bit looser or tighter, but then I wonder if I’m just paying too much attention or imagining it because you’re asking. So… no significant weight change that I can confidently point to.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced a lack of energy or fatigue most of the day?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I do feel tired a lot of the time, but I don’t know if I can honestly say it’s “most of the day” in the way you mean it.\n\nSome days I sit down and feel like I could just stay there indefinitely, but then I wonder whether that’s actual physical fatigue or more that I don’t want to start anything. It’s hard to separate the two. At my age, too, I sometimes second-guess myself — I think, well, maybe everyone feels a bit slower or more worn out than they used to.\n\nI haven’t noticed something like suddenly needing to nap all day, or collapsing from exhaustion, if that’s what you’re asking. It’s more vague than that. A heaviness, maybe. But I don’t have a clear, confident yes-or-no answer.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your depression is mild, or is it interfering with your daily life?\n\nPatient: No, I wouldn’t call it mild. I wish I could, but it’s gone well past just feeling low and still carrying on as usual.\n\nIt’s interfering with ordinary things now — things I used to manage without thinking. My wife has had to take over the finances, which is… embarrassing to say, because I’ve always been capable with that sort of thing. Bills, accounts, keeping track of what’s due — that used to be part of my routine. Now it’s like I can’t reliably handle it, and she’s had to step in.\n\nAnd she’s been staying with me more, really watching over me, for safety. I don’t like needing that. I don’t like admitting that she feels she has to do that.\n\nI’ve also stopped showing up reliably for things. The museum, the arts council, friends — I used to be dependable. If I said I’d be somewhere, I’d be there. Now I cancel, or I just don’t make it, or I can’t bring myself to answer people properly. So it’s not mild. It’s affecting my life in a very real way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your low mood or depression is present nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to pin that down. It feels like it’s there a lot, but “nearly every day” makes me hesitate, because I don’t want to overstate it or give you an answer that sounds neater than it really is.\n\nThere are days where I wake up and it’s just… already there, before anything has even happened. And then other days I might not notice it in the same obvious way, or I’ll get distracted for a while, and I think, well, does that count as it not being present? Or is it still there underneath and I’m just not paying attention to it for an hour?\n\nSo I suppose I’d say it’s frequent enough that it feels like part of the background of my life at the moment, but I can’t honestly give you a precise “yes, every single day” sort of answer. It’s hard for me to judge cleanly.\n\nDoctor: How many days in the past week have you felt depressed or had a low mood?\n\nPatient: I wish I could give you a clean number, but I’m honestly not sure. If I try to look back over the last week, the days sort of blur together a bit.\n\nI’d say it was several days, probably most of them in some form, but I don’t know whether each day would count as “depressed” in the way you’re asking. Some days it’s very obvious, and other days it’s more like a dull background feeling that I only notice when I stop moving or when things get quiet.\n\nSo… I can’t confidently say, “It was exactly five” or “exactly six.” It’s been there enough that the week feels colored by it, but I don’t have a precise count.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, with the low mood and loss of interest in activities?\n\nPatient: It’s been… about nine months now, I think. Not just a few bad weeks. It really seemed to settle in after those plans fell apart, and since then I’ve been sort of withdrawn and stuck in this low, guilty place.\n\nAt first I told myself I was just disappointed and that I’d pull myself together once the dust settled, but it didn’t lift. If anything, it became the way I was living day to day — not taking interest in things I used to care about, pulling back from people, feeling weighed down by it. So yes, roughly nine months. That’s the best estimate I can give.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your depression is severely impairing your ability to function in daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think I have to say yes, even though part of me wants to soften it or explain it away. It’s not just that I feel low and still manage everything quietly. It has really changed what I can do day to day.\n\nMy wife has had to take over the finances, which is hard for me to admit. I used to be the one who could keep track of things, pay attention to details, make sure bills and accounts were handled properly. Now it feels like too much, and she’s had to step in because I’m not reliable with it.\n\nAnd she stays with me for safety, too. That sounds terrible to say out loud, but it’s true. I don’t like needing that kind of watching over, especially at my age. It makes me feel ashamed and dependent, but I can see why she feels she has to.\n\nI’ve also stopped showing up reliably for things I used to be involved in — the museum, the arts council, even seeing friends. I don’t mean that I miss one thing here or there. It’s become a pattern. People probably don’t know whether I’m coming anymore, and honestly sometimes I don’t either. I just withdraw or fail to follow through.\n\nSo yes, I’d call it severely impairing. It’s not just internal anymore. It’s affecting my responsibilities, my marriage, my commitments, and my friendships.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly than usual or feeling like you're speeding up in your thoughts or actions?\n\nPatient: Yes… both, oddly enough. Or at least that’s how it looks from the outside, according to my wife.\n\nShe says there are times when I’m pacing through the house, moving from room to room, carrying papers around or shuffling them like I’m late for some board meeting I’ve forgotten about. I’m not always accomplishing anything when I do it. It’s more like I can’t quite settle, and I keep picking things up, putting them down, checking them, rearranging them.\n\nAnd then other times it’s the opposite. I’ll sit at the kitchen table and just sort of… freeze there. My wife will ask me something simple, nothing complicated, and it takes me a long time to answer. Not because I’m trying to be difficult. It’s like the question has to travel a long distance before I can respond. I can feel myself being slow, but I can’t always make myself speed up.\n\nSo yes, I have noticed it, but she notices it more clearly than I do. It seems to swing between this restless pacing and then sitting so still that I barely seem present.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving or speaking more slowly than usual?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I realized at first, actually.\n\nMy wife notices it most. She says sometimes I’ll sit at the kitchen table almost completely still, and when she asks me something simple, it takes me a long time to answer. I can hear her, but it’s like I’m delayed somehow, like I have to pull the words up through mud before I can say anything. It’s embarrassing, because I know it probably looks like I’m ignoring her or not trying, but I’m not.\n\nAnd then other times I’m not slow exactly — I’ll be up pacing through the house, shuffling papers around, almost like I’m late for some board meeting I’ve forgotten about. But when I stop and look at what I’m doing, I’m not really getting anywhere. It’s just this restless moving around.\n\nSo yes, especially with speaking slowly or taking too long to respond. That part has definitely been happening.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any days in the past two weeks where you felt completely free of depressive symptoms?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I can point to a whole day like that, no. I mean, “completely free” is a pretty strong phrase, and I don’t want to overstate it either way.\n\nThere may be moments where I’m distracted or where things feel a little lighter for an hour or two, but if you’re asking whether I’ve had an entire day in the past couple of weeks where I woke up and went through the day without that depressed feeling being there somewhere in the background… I don’t think so.\n\nIt’s more like it changes in intensity. Some parts of the day are worse than others, and sometimes I can almost forget it briefly if something occupies me, but it hasn’t really lifted in a clean way. I wouldn’t say I’ve felt completely free of it.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods in the past two weeks where your depressive symptoms were significantly worse than others?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can divide it up that neatly. It hasn’t felt like, say, Tuesday was dramatically different from Wednesday in a way I could mark on a calendar.\n\nThere are definitely parts of days that feel heavier than others, but I’m not sure I’d call them distinct “periods” where everything got significantly worse. It’s more like the whole thing is there, and it shifts around a bit — sometimes more in the foreground, sometimes more like background noise that still affects everything.\n\nI wish I had kept better track, honestly, because when you ask it that way I find myself trying to reconstruct the last two weeks and it all blurs together. So… I’d say I’m not really sure. I haven’t noticed a clear pattern of certain periods being much worse than the rest.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of self-harm in addition to thoughts of death or suicide?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have. I don’t like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nThere have been mornings where I wake up and almost immediately think it would have been easier if I just hadn’t woken up at all. Not in a dramatic way, exactly — more like this heavy sense of relief at the idea of not having to keep going through the day. And that scared me, because it felt too close to something I shouldn’t be sitting with by myself.\n\nI told my wife, actually. I told her I didn’t think I should be left alone. That was… hard to say to her, but I was frightened by where my mind was going."
